obtained in the pretraining process. For the downstream task, the input mask
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}
is the class encoding of the target variable
y_{\mathtt{DS}}
.

With the full input sequence
X
replaced by the truncated input sequence
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, the attention neural network
\widehat{f}_{\mathtt{DS}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}})
obtained in the SSL process has the same decomposition of the excess risk as that in (
5.3
). In the risk decomposition, for the SSL process, we have the same characterization of the generalization error and the optimization error as those in the supervised setting. When the downstream task is trained using the same set of truncated input sequences as that in the pretraining process, our previous analysis of the generalization error in the supervised setting is applicable to the SSL process. On the other hand, when the downstream task is trained using an independent set of truncated input sequences, we can modify our previous analysis to prove that the generalization error only scales with the complexity measure (e.g., the covering number) of the function class
\{\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{\mathtt{DS}}\}
of aggregation layers without depending on that of the attention mechanism, as
\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}
is frozen. Also, the attention neural network
\widehat{f}_{\mathtt{PT}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}})
obtained in the pretraining process has the same approximation error as that in the supervised setting. To characterize the approximation error for the SSL process, we analyze the approximation error for the downstream task by connecting it to the approximation error for the pretraining process.

Approximation Error.
In parallel to the supervised setting, we define the regression function and the latent-to-target mapping for the pretraining process as follows,

\displaystyle f_{\mathtt{PT}}^{*}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=\mathbb{E}[y_{\mathtt{PT}}{\,|\,}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu],\qquad g^{*}_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})=\mathbb{E}[y_{\mathtt{PT}}{\,|\,}{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},z].

(6.3)

Correspondingly, we defined the regression function and the latent-to-target mapping for the downstream task as follows,

\displaystyle f_{\mathtt{DS}}^{*}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=\mathbb{E}[y_{\mathtt{DS}}{\,|\,}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu],\qquad g_{\mathtt{DS}}^{*}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})=\mathbb{E}[y_{\mathtt{DS}}{\,|\,}{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}},z].

(6.4)

In parallel to the reweighted CME attention defined in (
5.6
), we defined the surrogate functions for the pretraining process and the downstream task as follows,

\displaystyle f_{W_{\mathtt{PT}}}^{\dagger}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})

\displaystyle=W_{\mathtt{PT}}^{\top}\mathtt{attn}_{\mathtt{CME}}\bigl(q_{*}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}),k_{*}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu),v_{*}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\bigr),

\displaystyle f_{W_{\mathtt{DS}}}^{\dagger}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})

\displaystyle=W_{\mathtt{DS}}^{\top}\mathtt{attn}_{\mathtt{CME}}\bigl(q_{*}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}),k_{*}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu),v_{*}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\bigr),

(6.5)

where
W_{\mathtt{PT}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
W_{\mathtt{DS}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{y}}}
are the reweighting parameters. We use the surrogate function to bridge the regression function and the attention neural network, which is illustrated in Figure
7
. In parallel to the latent-to-value mapping
\psi(z;{\mathtt{msk}})
defined in (
5.2
), we define the latent-to-value mappings for the pretraining process and the downstream task as follows,

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})=\mathbb{E}[v^{{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}}\,|\,{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},z],\qquad\psi_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})=\mathbb{E}[v^{{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}}\,|\,{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}},z],

where
v^{{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}}
and
v^{{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}}
replace
r^{\mathtt{msk}}
in (
4.2
). The latent-to-value mappings induce the kernel functions as follows,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathtt{PT}}(z,z^{\prime};{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})^{\top}\psi_{\mathtt{PT}}(z^{\prime};{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathtt{DS}}(z,z^{\prime};{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})^{\top}\psi_{\mathtt{DS}}(z^{\prime};{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}),

which induce the RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{PT}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}}
. Corresponding to (
5.8
), we have

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{PT}}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})

\displaystyle=\int\underbrace{W_{\mathtt{PT}}^{\top}\psi_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})}_{\displaystyle g_{W_{\mathtt{PT}}}^{\dagger}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})}\cdot\mathbb{P}(z\,|\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu){\mathrm{d}}z=\mathbb{E}_{z{\,|\,}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\bigl[g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{PT}}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})\bigr],

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{DS}}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})

\displaystyle=\int\underbrace{W_{\mathtt{DS}}^{\top}\psi_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})}_{\displaystyle g_{W_{\mathtt{DS}}}^{\dagger}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})}\cdot\mathbb{P}(z\,|\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu){\mathrm{d}}z=\mathbb{E}_{z{\,|\,}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\bigl[g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{DS}}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\bigr].

(6.6)

Note that
f^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{PT}}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
and
f^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{DS}}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
share the same latent posterior since the attention mechanism is frozen for the downstream task. By our previous arguments following (
5.8
)-(
5.10
), it remains to characterize how the reweighted CME attentions in (
6
) recover the latent-to-target mappings in (
6.3
)-(
6.4
) within the RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{PT}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}}
. See Figure
7
for an illustration of the construction of the RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{PT}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}}
.

Figure 7
:
The RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{PT}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}}
induced by the latent-to-value mappings
\psi_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
and
\psi_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
, respectively. The input masks
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}
and
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}
describe the pretraining process and the downstream task, respectively, and determine the RKHSs correspondingly. The
\ell_{\infty}
-norm projections
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{PT}},\infty}
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}},\infty}
are defined in Assumption
6.1
.

In parallel to Assumption
5.4
, we introduce the following assumption on the fundamental hardness of approximating the latent-to-target mappings within the RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{PT}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}}
.

Assumption 6.1

(SSL Recovery Gap)
.

For any fixed input masks
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}
and
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}
, let

\displaystyle g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{PT}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{PT}}},\infty}\bigl(g^{*}_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})\bigr)=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{g_{i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})\in\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{PT}}}}\bigl\|g^{*}_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})-g_{i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})\bigr\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{DS}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{DS}}},\infty}\bigl(g^{*}_{{\mathtt{DS}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\bigr)=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{g_{i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\in\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{DS}}}}\bigl\|g^{*}_{{\mathtt{DS}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})-g_{i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\bigr\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{SSL}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{DS}}},\infty}\bigl(g^{*}_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})\bigr)=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{g_{i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\in\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{DS}}}}\bigl\|g^{*}_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})-g_{i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\bigr\|_{\infty}

be the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm projections of the
i
-th entry
g^{*}_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
of the latent-to-target mapping
g^{*}_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
onto the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{PT}}}
, the
i
-th entry
g^{*}_{{\mathtt{DS}},i}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
of the latent-to-target mapping
g^{*}_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
onto the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{DS}}}
, and the
i
-th entry
g^{*}_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
of the latent-to-target mapping
g^{*}_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
onto the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathtt{DS}}}
, respectively. We assume the following statements hold.

(
{\mathtt{PT}}
)

There exists
\epsilon_{g}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})\in[0,+\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\bigl\|g_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}^{*}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})-g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{PT}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})\bigr\|^{2}_{\infty}\leq\epsilon_{g}^{2}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}).

(6.7)

(
{\mathtt{DS}}
)

There exists
\epsilon_{g}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\in[0,+\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d_{\mathrm{y}}}\bigl\|g_{{\mathtt{DS}},i}^{*}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})-g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{DS}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\bigr\|^{2}_{\infty}\leq\epsilon_{g}^{2}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}).

(6.8)

(
{\mathtt{SSL}}
)

There exists
\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\in[0,+\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\bigl\|g_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}^{*}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})-g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{SSL}},i}(\cdot;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\bigr\|^{2}_{\infty}\leq\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}^{2}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}).

(6.9)

Here the
\ell_{\infty}
-norms are taken over the latent variable
z
.

Intuitively, the feature
\psi_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
is obtained in the pretraining process, while the feature
\psi_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
is desired by the downstream task. Meanwhile, (
6.9
) characterizes the fundamental hardness of recovering the latent-to-target mapping
g_{\mathtt{PT}}^{*}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
for the pretraining process within the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}}
. Thus, the transfer error
\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
captures the transfer capability of the sequence embedding obtained in the pretraining process to the downstream task. In other words, when the pretraining process is sufficiently related to the downstream task, the transfer error
\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
is small, which allows us to approximate the
i
-th entry of the latent-to-target mapping
g^{*}_{\mathtt{PT}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
within the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}}
up to the approximation error
\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
.

We introduce the following assumption on the condition number that characterizes the alignment between the reweighting parameter desired by the downstream task and the reweighting parameter obtained in the pretraining process.

Assumption 6.2

(SSL Condition Number)
.

Let
\{g_{W_{\mathtt{DS}},i}^{\dagger}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})=w_{{\mathtt{DS}},i}^{\top}\psi_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\}_{i\in[d]}
and
\{g^{\dagger}_{W_{\mathtt{SSL}},i}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})=w_{{\mathtt{SSL}},i}^{\top}\psi_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\}_{i\in[d_{\mathrm{y}}]}
be the function classes satisfying (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) in Assumption
6.1
, respectively. Also, let
W_{\mathtt{DS}}=[w_{{\mathtt{DS}},1},\ldots,w_{{\mathtt{DS}},d_{\mathrm{y}}}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{y}}}
,
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}=[w_{{\mathtt{SSL}},1},\ldots,w_{{\mathtt{SSL}},d}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, and
2
2

2

For ease of presentation, we assume that
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}W^{\top}_{\mathtt{SSL}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is invertible. When
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}W^{\top}_{\mathtt{SSL}}
is not invertible, our subsequent analysis can be generalized using the pseudoinverse of
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}W^{\top}_{\mathtt{SSL}}
.

\displaystyle B=W_{\mathtt{DS}}^{\top}(W_{\mathtt{SSL}}W^{\top}_{\mathtt{SSL}})^{-1}W_{\mathtt{SSL}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{y}}\times d}.

(6.10)

We assume that there exists
\mu\in[0,+\infty)
such that
\|B\|_{2}^{2}\leq\mu
.

The condition number
\mu
plays a critical role in our subsequent analysis. To see the intuition behind
\mu
, let
W_{\mathtt{DS}}=W_{\mathtt{SSL}}
, which implies that
B
is a projection matrix and
\mu=1
. Also, let the row vectors of
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which implies that
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}W_{\mathtt{SSL}}^{\top}=I_{d_{\mathrm{p}}}
and
B=W_{\mathtt{DS}}^{\top}W_{\mathtt{SSL}}
. In this case,
B
measures the subspace alignment between the reweighting parameter
W_{\mathtt{DS}}
desired by the downstream task and the reweighting parameter
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}
obtained in the pretraining process. In general cases where
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}
is nonorthonormal, we have a similar interpretation through the eigenvalue decomposition of
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}W_{\mathtt{SSL}}^{\top}
.

Recall that
\widehat{f}_{\mathtt{PT}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
is the attention neural network obtained in the pretraining process. For any
U\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{y}}\times d}
, we define the following quantity that characterizes the expressity of the function class
\{\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{\mathtt{DS}}\}
of aggregation layers for the downstream task,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(U)

\displaystyle=\inf_{f_{\mathtt{DS}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{DS}}}\sup_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mathfrak{X}^{L-1}}\bigl\|f_{\mathtt{DS}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})-U\widehat{f}_{\mathtt{PT}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{\mathtt{DS}}}\sup_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mathfrak{X}^{L-1}}\bigl\|\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{\theta}\circ\mathtt{attn}_{\mathtt{SM}}\bigl(q_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}),k_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu),v_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad-U\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{PT}}_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}\circ\mathtt{attn}_{\mathtt{SM}}\bigl(q_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}),k_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu),v_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\bigr)\bigr\|_{2},

(6.11)

where
\mathfrak{X}^{L-1}
is defined in (
5.2
). Since the attention mechanism is frozen for the downstream task, the trainable part of the attention neural network is the aggregation layer
\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{\theta}
. Thus, the aggregation approximation error
\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(U)
characterizes the expressity of the function class
\{\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta_{\mathtt{DS}}\}
of aggregation layers in terms of approximating the composition of (i) the linear transformation
U\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{PT}}_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}
of the aggregation layer obtained in the pretraining process and (ii) the output variation induced by switching the input mask
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}
to another input mask
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}
in the attention mechanism, which is frozen. To see the intuition behind
\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(U)
, let
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}={\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}
, which implies that
\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(U)=0
as long as the function class of aggregation layers takes the form of
\{\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{\theta}=U\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{PT}}_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}:\theta=U\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{y}}\times d}\}
. In this case,
\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(U)
characterizes the compatibility between
\mathtt{agg}_{\theta}^{\mathtt{DS}}
and
\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{PT}}_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}
under a linear transformation parameterized by
\theta
. In general cases where
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}\neq{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}
,
\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(U)
additionally characterizes the capability of
\mathtt{agg}_{\theta}^{\mathtt{DS}}
to capture the output variation induced by switching the input mask.

Recall that the function class
\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{PT}}
of attention neural networks for the pretraining process is defined in (
6.1
). Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rm approx}^{\mathtt{PT}}=\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{PT}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{L}\bigl((\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,y_{\mathtt{PT}}),f\bigr)\Bigr]-\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{L}\bigl((\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,y_{\mathtt{PT}}),f_{\mathtt{PT}}^{*}\bigr)\Bigr]

(6.12)

be the approximation error for the pretraining process, which is characterized in Theorem
5.5
. Recall that the function class
\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{DS}}
of attention neural networks for the downstream task is defined in (
6.2
). For the downstream task, the approximation error in (
5.3
) takes the following form,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rm approx}=\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{DS}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{L}\bigl((\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,y_{\mathtt{DS}}),f\bigr)\Bigr]-\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathcal{L}\bigl((\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,y_{\mathtt{DS}}),f_{\mathtt{DS}}^{*}\bigr)\Bigr].

(6.13)

The following theorem characterizes the approximation error
\mathcal{E}_{\rm approx}
for the SSL process.

Theorem 6.3

(SSL Approximation Error)
.

Under Assumptions
6.1
and
6.2
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\text{approx}}=O\Bigl(\mu\cdot\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{\rm approx}^{{\mathtt{PT}}}+\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}^{2}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})\bigr)+\epsilon_{g}^{2}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})+\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}^{2}(B)\Bigr),

where
\mathcal{E}^{\mathtt{PT}}_{\rm approx}
,
\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
,
\epsilon_{g}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
, and
\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(B)
are defined in (
6.12
), (
6.9
), (
6.8
), and (
6
), respectively, and
B
is defined in (
6.10
).

Proof.

See §
E.3
for a detailed proof.
∎

Theorem
6.3
demonstrates that the attention neural network enables the transfer capability to diverse downstream tasks, where the approximation error is subsume from that in the supervised setting with a few extra error terms. We interpret the approximation error in Theorem
6.3
as follows.

(i)

The condition number
\mu
characterizes the the alignment between the reweighting parameter
W_{\mathtt{DS}}
desired by the downstream task and the reweighting parameter
W_{\mathtt{SSL}}
obtained in the pretraining process. When
W_{\mathtt{DS}}=W_{\mathtt{SSL}}
, we have
\mu=1
.

(ii)

The approximation error
\mathcal{E}_{\rm approx}^{\mathtt{PT}}
for the pretraining process is characterized in Theorem
5.5
. Specifically,
\mathcal{E}_{\rm approx}^{\mathtt{PT}}
involves the pretraining recovery gap
\epsilon_{g}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
defined in (
6.7
), which characterizes the fundamental hardness of approximating the
i
-th entry
g_{{\mathtt{PT}},i}^{*}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
of the latent-to-target mapping
g_{\mathtt{PT}}^{*}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}})
defined in (
6.3
) within the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{PT}}
, and the attention approximation error
\epsilon_{\mathtt{attn}}
defined in (
5.11
), which is characterized in Proposition
4.2
.

(iii)

The transfer error
\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
captures the transfer capability of the sequence embedding obtained in the pretraining process to the downstream task. By our previous arguments following Assumption
6.1
,
\epsilon_{\mathtt{SSL}}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}},{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
is small as long as the pretraining process is sufficiently related to the downstream task.

(iv)

The downstream recovery gap
\epsilon_{g}({\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
defined in (
6.8
) characterizes the fundamental hardness of approximating the
i
-th entry
g_{{\mathtt{DS}},i}^{*}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
of the latent-to-target mapping
g^{*}_{\mathtt{DS}}(z;{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}})
defined in (
6.4
) within the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mathtt{DS}}
.

(v)

The aggregation approximation error
\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(B)
measures the expressity of the function class of aggregation layers for the downstream task. By our previous arguments following (
6
),
\epsilon_{\mathtt{agg}}(B)
is small as long as the aggregation layer
\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{DS}}_{\theta}
for the downstream task can approximate the composition of the linear transformation
B\mathtt{agg}^{\mathtt{PT}}_{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathtt{PT}}}
of the aggregation layer obtained in the pretraining process and the variation induced by switching the input mask
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{PT}}
to another input mask
{\mathtt{msk}}_{\mathtt{DS}}
.

7
Conclusion

The attention mechanism in transformers demonstrates significant empirical successes in natural language processing and computer vision, but there is a lack of understanding about how it works and why it is effective. To this end, we answer three questions about the attention mechanism: (i) what makes a good representation, (ii) how the attention mechanism produces this representation during the forward pass, and (iii) how the attention mechanism learns to produce this representation during the backward pass. Through the lens of exchangeability, we provide a theoretical characterization of the attention mechanism as a combination of a “whitebox” design guided by a latent variable model and a “blackbox” design allowing for learnable parameters. Also, we establish the approximation, generalization, and optimization guarantees of the attention mechanism. Several challenging questions remain open, e.g., what is the theoretical benefit of a multilayer composition of many attention mechanisms and how it affects the approximation guarantee.

8

Zhaoran Wang acknowledges National Science Foundation (Awards 2048075, 2008827, 2015568, 1934931), Simons Institute (Theory of Reinforcement Learning), Amazon, J.P. Morgan, and Two Sigma for their supports.

Appendix A
Conditional Mean Embedding

We introduce the conditional mean embedding
(
Song et al. 2009
)
, which embeds a conditional distribution to an element in an RKHS. Let
\mathcal{H}_{x}
and
\mathcal{H}_{y}
be the two RKHSs over the spaces
{\mathfrak{X}}
and
{\mathfrak{Y}}
with the kernels
\mathfrak{K}
and
\mathfrak{L}
, respectively. We denote by
\phi:{\mathfrak{X}}\rightarrow\ell_{2}
and
\varphi:{\mathfrak{Y}}\rightarrow\ell_{2}
the feature mappings associated with
\mathcal{H}_{x}
and
\mathcal{H}_{y}
, respectively. In other words, it holds for any
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
and
y,y^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{Y}}
that

\displaystyle\phi(x)^{\top}\phi(x^{\prime})=\mathfrak{K}(x,x^{\prime}),\qquad\varphi(y)^{\top}\varphi(y)=\mathfrak{L}(y,y^{\prime}).

(A.1)

Let
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}
be the joint distribution of the two random variables
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
taking values in
{\mathfrak{X}}
and
{\mathfrak{Y}}
, respectively. The conditional mean embedding
{\mathtt{CME}}(x,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}})\in\mathcal{H}_{y}
of the conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}}
is defined as

\displaystyle{\mathtt{CME}}(x,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}})=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{Y},\cdot){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{X}=x\bigr].

By the reproducing property, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[g(\mathcal{Y}){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{X}=x\bigr]=\big\langle g,{\mathtt{CME}}(x,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}})\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{y}},\quad\forall g\in\mathcal{H}_{y},x\in{\mathfrak{X}}.

Correspondingly, the conditional mean embedding operator
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{H}_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{y}
is a linear operator such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}}\mathfrak{K}(x,\cdot)={\mathtt{CME}}(x,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}),

for any
x\in{\mathfrak{X}}
.
We define the (uncentered) covariance operator
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{H}_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{x}
and the (uncentered) cross-covariance operator
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{H}_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{y}
as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{K}(\mathcal{X},\cdot)\otimes\mathfrak{K}(\mathcal{X},\cdot)\bigr],\qquad\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{Y},\cdot)\otimes\mathfrak{K}(\mathcal{X},\cdot)\bigr].

Here
\otimes
is the tensor product. As shown in
Song et al. 2009
, it holds that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}}^{-1}
. Thus, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathtt{CME}}(x,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}})=\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(x,\cdot).

(A.2)

To derive the empirical estimation of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}}
, we consider the following regularized least-squares problem,

\displaystyle\min_{\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{H}_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{y}}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{C})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\big\|\mathfrak{L}(y^{\ell},\cdot)-\mathcal{C}\mathfrak{K}(x^{\ell},\cdot)\big\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{y}}+\lambda\cdot\|\mathcal{C}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}},

(A.3)

where
\{(x^{\ell},y^{\ell})\}_{\ell\in[L]}
are independently and identically sampled from
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}
,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
denotes the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, and
\lambda>0
is the regularization parameter.
Recall from (
A.1
) that
\phi
and
\varphi
are the feature mappings associated with the RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{x}
and
\mathcal{H}_{y}
. To ease the presentation, we view the space
\ell_{2}
as an (infinite-dimensional) vector space and consider the feature mappings
\phi:{\mathfrak{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{\phi}}
and
\varphi:{\mathfrak{Y}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{\varphi}}
, where
d_{\phi}
and
d_{\varphi}
can be infinity.
We write
\phi(X)=(\phi(x^{1}),\ldots,\phi(x^{L}))^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\phi}}
and
\varphi(Y)=(\phi(y^{1}),\ldots,\phi(y^{L}))^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\varphi}}
.
Also, we define the the (uncentered) empirical covariance operator
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}}
and (uncentered) empirical cross-covariance operator
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}}
as follows,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\phi(x^{\ell})\phi(x^{\ell})^{\top}=L^{-1}\phi(X)^{\top}\phi(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\phi}\times d_{\phi}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\varphi(y^{\ell})\varphi(x^{\ell})^{\top}=L^{-1}\varphi(Y)\phi(X)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\varphi}\times d_{\phi}}.

(A.4)

Then, the solution to (
A.3
) is

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}}^{\lambda}=\varphi(Y)^{\top}\phi(X)\bigl(\phi(X)^{\top}\phi(X)+\lambda\mathcal{I}\bigr)^{-1}=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\varphi}\times d_{\phi}}.

We denote by
\widehat{\mathtt{CME}}_{\lambda}(x,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}})=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}}\phi(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\varphi}}
the empirical conditional mean embedding.
Note that

\displaystyle\phi(X)\bigl(\phi(X)^{\top}\phi(X)+\lambda\mathcal{I}\bigr)^{-1}=\bigl(\phi(X)\phi(X)^{\top}+\lambda I\bigr)^{-1}\phi(X).

Thus, it holds that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathtt{CME}}_{\lambda}(x,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}}^{\lambda}\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{X}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(x,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\varphi(Y)^{\top}\phi(X)\bigl(\phi(X)^{\top}\phi(X)+\lambda\mathcal{I}\bigr)^{-1}\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\varphi(Y)^{\top}\bigl(\phi(X)\phi(X)^{\top}+\lambda I\bigr)^{-1}\phi(X)\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\varphi(Y)^{\top}(\mathfrak{K}(X,X)+\lambda I)^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(X,x).

(A.5)

Here
\mathfrak{K}(X,X)=\phi(X)\phi(X)^{\top}=(\mathfrak{K}(x^{i},x^{j}))_{i,j\in[L]}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}
is the Gram matrix and
\mathfrak{K}(X,x)=\phi(X)\phi(x)=(\mathfrak{K}(x^{1},x),\ldots,\mathfrak{K}(x^{L},x))\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
.

Appendix B
Attention Recovers Latent Posterior

B.1
Gaussian Process Regression

Gaussian Process Regression.

We say that
f
follows a Gaussian process
\mathtt{GP}(\mu,\mathfrak{K})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
if for any
x^{1},\ldots,x^{L}
,
(f(x^{1}),\ldots,f(x^{L}))
follows a Gaussian distribution with mean
(\mu(x^{1}),\ldots,\mu(x^{L}))
and covariance
(\mathfrak{K}(x^{i},x^{j}))_{i,j\in[L]}
.
Here
\mu(x)=\mathbb{E}[f(x)]
is the mean function and
\mathfrak{K}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[(f(x)-\mu(x))(f(x^{\prime})-\mu(x^{\prime}))]
is the covariance (or kernel) function, where
f
is random.
We take
\mathtt{GP}(0,\mathfrak{K})
as the prior of
f
.
Given a dataset
\mathcal{D}=\{(x^{\ell},y^{\ell})\}_{\ell\in[L]}
from the regression model
y^{\ell}=f(x^{\ell})+\epsilon^{\ell}
with
\epsilon^{\ell}\sim N(0,\lambda I)
, the posterior of
f
is a Gaussian process with mean
\mu_{\mathcal{D}}(x)
and covariance
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,x^{\prime})

(
Schulz et al. 2018
)
as follows,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{D}}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{K}(x,X)\bigl(\mathfrak{K}(X,X)+\lambda I\bigr)^{-1}Y,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\mathcal{D}}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{K}(x,x^{\prime})-\mathfrak{K}(x,X)\bigl(\mathfrak{K}(X,X)+\lambda I\bigr)^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(X,x^{\prime}).

Here
\mathfrak{K}(x,X)=(\mathfrak{K}(x,x^{\ell}))_{\ell\in[L]}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times L}
,
\mathfrak{K}(X,X)=(\mathfrak{K}(x^{i},x^{j}))_{i,j\in[L]}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}
,
\mathfrak{K}(X,x^{\prime})=(\mathfrak{K}(x^{\ell},x^{\prime}))_{\ell\in[L]}\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
, and
Y=(y^{1},\ldots,y^{L})\in\mathbb{R}^{L}

Rigorous Characterization of Latent Variable Model.

We provide a rigorous characterization of the advanced infinite-dimensional example of the latent variable model in §
4.1
.We consider the following model,

\displaystyle r^{\ell}=f(c^{\ell})+\epsilon^{\ell},\qquad r^{\mathtt{msk}}=f(c^{\mathtt{msk}})+\epsilon.

(B.1)

Here
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
with
f_{i}\sim\mathtt{GP}(0,\mathfrak{K}(\cdot,\cdot))
for any
i\in[d]
and
\epsilon^{\ell}
and
\epsilon
are independent Gaussian noises drawn from
N(0,\lambda I)
. Then, following the Gaussian process regression, we recover (
4.1
) as the mean of the posterior of the Gaussian process.

B.2
Implication of Convergence with
L\rightarrow\infty

Necessity of Multiple Heads.

Based on the definition of the attention mechanism
\mathtt{attn}
in (
2.1
), we define the multihead attention as

\displaystyle{\mathtt{mha}}(q,X;W)=\sum_{i=1}^{h}{\mathtt{head}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(B.2)

Here
h\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is the head number,
W=\{(W_{i}^{\mathrm{q}},W_{i}^{\mathrm{k}},W_{i}^{\mathrm{v}})\}_{i=1}^{h}
with
W_{i}^{\mathrm{q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
,
W_{i}^{\mathrm{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
, and
W_{i}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the learnable parameter, and

\displaystyle{\mathtt{head}}_{i}=\mathtt{attn}(q,K_{i},V_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad\text{where}\quad K_{i}=XW_{i}^{\mathrm{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{p}}},\quad V_{i}=XW_{i}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}.

In the multihead attention, we set
d=d_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot h
, where
h
is the head number and
d_{\mathrm{p}}
is the dimension of the key and the query.
We remark that the multihead attention defined in (
B.2
) is written in the summation form, which is equivalent to the concatenation form
(
Vaswani et al. 2017
)
. To see this, we consider the concatenation form of the multihead attention,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathtt{mha}}(q,X;\widetilde{W})=\bigl((W^{\mathrm{o}}_{1})^{\top},\ldots,(W^{\mathrm{o}}_{h})^{\top}\bigr)\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\mathtt{head}}_{1}\\
\ldots\\
\widetilde{\mathtt{head}}_{h}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}(W^{\mathrm{o}}_{i})^{\top}\widetilde{\mathtt{head}}_{i},

(B.3)

where
W^{\mathrm{o}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{p}}\times d}
is a learnable parameter with the
i
-th block
W^{\mathrm{o}}_{i}
and the
i
-th head
\widetilde{\mathtt{head}}_{i}
is obtained via

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathtt{head}}_{i}=\mathtt{attn}(q,K_{i},\widetilde{V}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{p}}},\qquad\text{where}\quad K_{i}=XW_{i}^{\mathrm{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{p}}},\quad V_{i}=X\widetilde{W}_{i}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}.

Here
\widetilde{W}_{i}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
We see that the (
B.2
) and (
B.3
) are equivalent when
{\mathtt{head}}_{i}=(W^{\mathrm{o}}_{i})^{\top}\widetilde{\mathtt{head}}_{i}
, which holds when
W_{i}^{\mathrm{v}}=\widetilde{W}_{i}^{\mathrm{v}}W^{\mathrm{o}}_{i}
.

We use
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}=q]
to demonstrate the necessity of using multiple heads in the multihead attention.
Note that the key and value are obtained by the following mappings,

\displaystyle k^{\ell}=(W^{\mathrm{k}})^{\top}x^{\ell},\qquad v^{\ell}=(W^{\mathrm{v}})^{\top}x^{\ell},

where
x^{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the input token and
W^{\mathrm{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
,
W^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are the learnable parameters.
We consider a single-head attention, where
h=1
,
d_{\mathrm{p}}=d
, and
W^{\mathrm{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is invertible.
We denote by
\mathcal{K}
,
\mathcal{V}
, and
\mathcal{X}
the random variable with the same distribution as
k^{\ell}
,
v^{\ell}
, and
x^{\ell}
, respectively. By Propositions
4.1
and
4.2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{attn}(q,K,V)\approx\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}=q]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(W^{\mathrm{v}})^{\top}\mathcal{X}\,\big|\,(W^{\mathrm{k}})^{\top}\mathcal{X}=q\bigr]=\bigl((W^{\mathrm{k}})^{-1}W^{\mathrm{v}}\bigr)^{\top}q,

which is a linear mapping and fails to capture the nonlinear interaction query
q
and the input sequence
X
.
In other words, the single-head attention becomes a linear mapping in the limit with
L\rightarrow\infty
.
In contrast, when
h>1
, we have
d_{\mathrm{p}}=d/h<d
, which implies that the matrix
W^{\mathrm{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is not invertible.
Thus, using multiple heads avoid the degenerating issue.

Connection to Instrumental Variable.

We draw a connection from the attention mechanism to the instrumental variable model.
Instrumental variable regression estimates the causal relationship between the input
\mathcal{X}
and the output
\mathcal{Y}
.
Specifically, when
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is confouneded, an instrumental variable
\mathcal{W}
is effective in identifying the causal relationship between
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
.
Intuitively,
\mathcal{W}
is an instrumental variable if it influences
\mathcal{Y}
only through
\mathcal{X}
which is formalized as follows.

Assumption B.1

(Instrumental Variable Model)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{W})
be a random variable on the space
{\mathfrak{X}}\times{\mathfrak{Y}}\times{\mathfrak{W}}
with joint distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{W}}
. We assume that

(i)

\mathcal{Y}=g(\mathcal{X})+\epsilon
and
\mathbb{E}[\epsilon{\,|\,}\mathcal{W}=w]=0
for any
w\in{\mathfrak{W}}
, and

(ii)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}{\,|\,}\mathcal{W}}(x{\,|\,}w)
does not remain when
w
varies.

Under Assumption
B.1
,
\mathcal{W}
is an instrumental variable.
Specifically, (i) of Assumption
B.1
is the exclusion restriction, where function
g
is the structural function of interest and
\epsilon
is the confounding noise.
Also, (ii) of Assumption
B.1
is the relevance condition, which ensures that
\mathcal{W}
is informative in the sense that it depends on
w
in a nontrivial manner.
We remark that the instrumental variable model generalizes the standard regression model.
To see this, when
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{W}
, the estimation of
g
reduces to standard regression of unconfounded inputs and it holds that
g(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{X}=\cdot]
. In particular, the instrumental variable model allows that
\mathcal{X}
and
\epsilon
are confounded, i.e.,
\mathcal{X}
and
\epsilon
are dependent.
By Assumption
B.1
, we have the following estimation equation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Y}{\,|\,}\mathcal{W}=w]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[g(\mathcal{X}){\,|\,}\mathcal{W}=w\bigr].

(B.4)

The right-hand side of (
B.4
) provides a two-stage method for estimating the function
g
. At the first stage, we estimate the conditional mean mean embedding of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}{\,|\,}\mathcal{W}}
. Then, at the second stage, we estimate the function
g
via regressing
\mathcal{Y}
on the empirical conditional mean mean embedding of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}{\,|\,}\mathcal{W}}

(
Singh et al. 2019
)
.

To ease the presentation, we consider the following mapping,

\displaystyle g_{\theta}\circ\mathtt{attn}(q,K,V)\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
g_{\theta}
is a function approximator with a learnable parameter
\theta
. For example,
g_{\theta}
is a linear or kernel function.
By Proposition
4.1
and Proposition
4.2
, it holds that

\displaystyle g_{\theta}\circ\mathtt{attn}(q,K,V)\approx g_{\theta}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}=q]\bigr),\qquad\text{as}\quad L\rightarrow\infty.

Let the target variable be
y
. Then, the learning objective takes the following form,

\displaystyle\min_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\big\|y-g_{\theta}(\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}=q])\big\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr],

which corresponds to the second stage of estimating the instrumental variable model. Note that
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}=q]
is the conditional mean embedding of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}}
.
Thus, the key
\mathcal{K}
can be viewed as the instrumental variable
(
Pearl 2009
)
, which handles the endogeneity.
We provide an alternative view on how the attention mechanism performs relational reasoning as a causal inference procedure.

B.3
Proof of Lemma
3.2

Proof.

First, we prove the statement that
b_{z}(X)=\mathbb{P}(z=\cdot{\,|\,}X)
is a minimal sufficient statistic of
X
for
z
. To see the sufficiency of
b_{z}(X)
for
z
, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(z{\,|\,}X)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(z{\,\big|\,}\mathbb{P}(z=\cdot{\,|\,}X)\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(z{\,\big|\,}b_{z}(X)\bigr).

To see
b_{z}(X)
is the minimal sufficient statistic, let
U(X)
be another sufficient statistic of
X
for
z
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(z\,|\,X)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(z{\,\big|\,}U(X)\bigr),

which implies that
b_{z}(X)
is a function of
U(X)
. Thus,
b_{z}(X)
is minimal.

Second, we prove the statement that
b_{z}(X)
is a minimal sufficient statistics of
X
for
y
. To see the sufficiency of
b_{z}(X)
for
y
, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(y{\,|\,}X)=\int\mathbb{P}(y{\,|\,}z)\cdot\mathbb{P}(z{\,|\,}X){\mathrm{d}}z,

which implies that
\mathbb{P}(y{\,|\,}X)=\mathbb{P}(y{\,|\,}b_{z}(X))
since
\mathbb{P}(y{\,|\,}X)
only depends on
X
through
b_{z}(X)
.
Suppose that
U(X)
is a sufficient statistic of
X
for
y
. We have

\displaystyle\int\mathbb{P}(y\,|\,z)\cdot\mathbb{P}(z\,|\,X){\mathrm{d}}z=\mathbb{P}(y\,|\,X)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl(y{\,\big|\,}U(X)\bigr)=\int\mathbb{P}(y\,|\,z)\cdot\mathbb{P}\bigl(z{\,\big|\,}U(X)\bigr){\mathrm{d}}z.

By the definition of
{\mathcal{T}}
in (
3.1
), we then have that

\displaystyle b_{z}(X)=\mathbb{P}(z=\cdot{\,|\,}X)={\mathcal{T}}^{-1}\Bigl(\int\mathbb{P}(y=\cdot\,|\,z)\cdot\mathbb{P}\bigl(z\,\big|\,U(X)\bigr){\mathrm{d}}z\Bigr),

which implies that
b_{z}(X)
is a function of
U(X)
. Thus,
b_{z}(X)
is minimal.
∎

B.4
Proof of Proposition
4.1

Proof.

For notational simplicity, we denote by
\|\cdot\|
the RKHS norm for elements in an RKHS and the operator norm for operators between two RKHSs. Also, we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{k}
and
\mathcal{H}_{v}
the RKHSs for the key and the value with the kernel functions
\mathfrak{K}
and
\mathfrak{L}
, respectively.
Note that we consider the Euclidean kernel
\mathfrak{L}(v,v^{\prime})=v^{\top}v^{\prime}
for the value, which uses the identity mapping
\varphi
as the feature mapping.
Recall the definition of the empirical covariance operator and the empirical cross-covariance operator in (
A
). Correspondingly, we write

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\phi(K)^{\top}\phi(K)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\phi}\times d_{\phi}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\varphi(V)^{\top}\phi(K)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\varphi}\times d_{\phi}},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=L^{-1}\varphi(V)^{\top}\varphi(V)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\varphi}\times d_{\varphi}}.

Here
\phi(K)=(\phi(k^{1}),\ldots,\phi(k^{L}))^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\phi}}
and
\varphi(V)=(\phi(v^{1}),\ldots,\phi(v^{L}))^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\varphi}}

By the definition of the CME attention in (
4.5
) and the equality in (
A
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathtt{attn}_{{\mathtt{CME}}}(q,K,V)=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\phi(q),

which implies that
\mathtt{attn}_{\mathtt{CME}}
recovers the empirical conditional mean embedding.
By (
A.2
), it holds that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathtt{attn}(q,K,V)-{\mathtt{CME}}(q,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{K},\mathcal{V}})\big\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\underbrace{\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\phi(q)-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\phi(q)\big\|}_{\displaystyle\text{(i)}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\big\|\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)\big\|}_{\displaystyle\text{(ii)}}.

(B.5)

Upper bounding term (i) of (
B.4
).

We adapt the proof from
Song et al. 2009
.
It suffices to upper bound
\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\|
. It holds that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|

(B.6)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}\bigl((\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}-(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\bigr)\Big\|+\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}})(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|

\displaystyle\quad=\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}})(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|+\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}})(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|.

For the first term on the right-hand side of (
B.6
), we have the operator decomposition that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{V}}^{1/2}\mathcal{W}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}^{1/2}
for
\mathcal{W}
such that
\|\mathcal{W}\|\leq 1
.
Then, we have that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}})(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{V}}\|^{1/2}\cdot\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}^{1/2}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1/2}\big\|\cdot\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1/2}\big\|\cdot\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}})(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1/2}\cdot\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}})(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|,

(B.7)

where the last inequality follows from

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{V}}\|^{2}=L^{-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\|v^{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1,\quad\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\leq\mathcal{I},\quad(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\leq(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{I}.

Plugging (
B.4
) into (
B.6
), we have

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|

(B.8)

\displaystyle\quad\leq(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1/2}\cdot\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}})(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|+\big\|(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}})(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|.

In what follows, we upper bound the second term on the right-hand side of (
B.8
) using Lemma
G.2
. We define
\xi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{p}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{v}
as follows,

\displaystyle\xi(k,v)=\varphi(v)\phi(k)^{\top}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}.

Since
\big\|(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|\leq(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1}
, we have that

\displaystyle\big\|\xi(k,v)\big\|=\big\|(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|\cdot\big\|\varphi(v)\big\|\cdot\big\|\phi(k)\big\|\leq C\cdot(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1},

where
C>0
is an absolute constant.
In addition, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\big\|\xi(k,v)\big\|^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\big\|\phi(k)^{\top}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|^{2}\cdot\big\|\varphi(v)\big\|^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\big\|(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\phi(k)\big\|^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\big\langle(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-2}\phi(k),\phi(k)\big\rangle\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\big\langle(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\phi(k),\phi(k)\big\rangle\Bigr].

Using the trace operator, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\big\|\xi(k,v)\big\|^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\operatorname{tr}\bigl((\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-2}\phi(k)\phi(k)^{\top}\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}\bigl((\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-2}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{tr}\bigl((\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1}\cdot\Gamma(L^{-1}\lambda).

Here
\Gamma(L^{-1}\lambda)
is the effective dimension of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}
, which is defined as follows,

\displaystyle\Gamma(L^{-1}\lambda)=\operatorname{tr}\bigl((\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\bigr).

Applying Lemma
G.2
with
B=C(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1}
and
\sigma^{2}=(L^{-1}\lambda)^{-1}\cdot\Gamma(L^{-1}\lambda)
, we have with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|\leq C\cdot\biggl(\frac{2}{\lambda}+\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(L^{-1}\lambda)}{\lambda}}\biggr)\log\frac{2}{\delta},

(B.9)

where
C>0
is an absolute constant. Similarly, we have with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\biggl(\frac{2}{\lambda}+\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(L^{-1}\lambda)}{\lambda}}\biggr)\log\frac{2}{\delta}.

(B.10)

Here
C^{\prime}>0
is an absolute constant.
Plugging (
B.9
) and (
B.10
) into (
B.8
), we have with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\big\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq C^{\prime\prime}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{L}{\lambda}}\cdot\biggl(\frac{2}{\lambda}+\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(L^{-1}\lambda)}{\lambda}}\biggr)\log\frac{2}{\delta}.

(B.11)

Upper bounding term (ii) of (
B.4
).

We adapt the proof from
Fukumizu 2015
.
For any
g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}g,\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}g\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{V},\bar{\mathcal{V}})g(\mathcal{K})g(\bar{\mathcal{K}})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{V},\bar{\mathcal{V}}){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{K},\bar{\mathcal{K}}\bigr]g(\mathcal{K})g(\bar{\mathcal{K}})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\Big\langle(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}})\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{V},\bar{\mathcal{V}}){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{K}=\cdot,\bar{\mathcal{K}}=\dagger\bigr],g\otimes g\Big\rangle.

Similarly, we have for any
q\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{p}}}
and any
g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
that

\displaystyle\Big\langle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}},\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{V},\cdot){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{K}=q\bigr]\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{V},\bar{\mathcal{V}}){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{K}=q,\mathcal{K}=\dagger\bigr],\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}g\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big\langle(\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}})\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{V},\bar{\mathcal{V}}){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{K}=\cdot,\bar{\mathcal{K}}=\dagger\bigr],\mathfrak{L}(\cdot,q)\otimes g\Big\rangle.

By setting
g=(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)
, we have that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)\big\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\big\langle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot),\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\bigg\langle\Big((\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{I}\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{I}\Big)\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{V},\bar{\mathcal{V}}){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{K}=\cdot,\bar{\mathcal{K}}=\dagger\bigr],\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)\otimes\mathfrak{K}(q,\dagger)\bigg\rangle.

Note that
\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{L}(v,\bar{v}){\,|\,}k=\cdot,\bar{k}=\dagger]\in\mathcal{H}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{k}
is in the range of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}
. We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}\times\mathcal{H}_{k}
such that
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}})\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}=\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{L}(v,\bar{v}){\,|\,}k=\cdot,\bar{k}=\dagger]
. Let
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
be the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}
, respectively. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)\big\|^{4}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\bigg\|\Big((\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}-\mathcal{I}\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+L^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{I}\Big)\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathfrak{L}(\mathcal{V},\bar{\mathcal{V}}){\,\big|\,}\mathcal{K}=\cdot,\bar{\mathcal{K}}=\dagger\bigr]\bigg\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{i,j}\biggl(\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+L^{-1}\lambda}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\lambda_{j}+L^{-1}\lambda}-\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{i}+L^{-1}\lambda}-\frac{\lambda_{j}^{2}\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}+L^{-1}\lambda}+\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\biggr)^{2}\cdot\langle\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi_{j},\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{i,j}\biggl(\frac{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}(L^{-1}\lambda)^{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+L^{-1}\lambda)(\lambda_{j}+L^{-1}\lambda)}\biggr)^{2}\cdot\langle\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi_{j},\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq(L^{-1}\lambda)^{4}\cdot\|\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\|^{2}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}+\lambda\mathcal{I})^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)-\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{K}\mathcal{K}}^{-1}\mathfrak{K}(q,\cdot)\big\|_{2}\leq C\cdot\lambda L^{-1},

(B.12)

where
C>0
is an absolute constant.

Plugging (
B.4
) and (
B.12
) into (
B.4
), we have with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathtt{attn}(q,K,V)-{\mathtt{CME}}(q,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{K},\mathcal{V}})\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\biggl(\sqrt{\frac{L}{\lambda}}\cdot\biggl(\frac{2}{\lambda}+\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(L^{-1}\lambda)}{\lambda}}\biggr)\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\lambda L^{-1}\biggr).

Thus, we complete the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

B.5
Proof of Proposition
4.2

Proof.

Under the condition that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\mathfrak{K}}_{\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}}(v{\,|\,}q)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(v{\,|\,}q)
uniformly for any
q\in\mathbb{S}^{d_{\mathrm{p}}-1}
as
L\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\int v\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\mathfrak{K}}_{\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}}(v{\,|\,}q){\mathrm{d}}v\rightarrow\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}=q]\qquad\text{as}\quad L\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover, it holds that

\displaystyle\int v\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{\mathfrak{K}}_{\mathcal{V}{\,|\,}\mathcal{K}}(v{\,|\,}q){\mathrm{d}}v

\displaystyle=\iota\cdot\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}v\cdot\frac{\sum^{L}_{\ell=1}\mathfrak{K}(k^{\ell},q)\cdot\mathfrak{K}(v^{\ell},v)}{\sum^{L}_{\ell=1}\mathfrak{K}(k^{\ell},q)}{\mathrm{d}}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\iota\cdot\sum^{L}_{\ell=1}\mathfrak{K}(k^{\ell},q)\cdot\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}v\cdot\mathfrak{K}(v^{\ell},v){\mathrm{d}}v}{\sum^{L}_{\ell=1}\mathfrak{K}(k^{\ell},q)},

(B.13)

where
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is the
(d-1)
-dimensional unit sphere. It suffices to calculate the integration term
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}v\cdot\mathfrak{K}(v^{\ell},v){\mathrm{d}}v
.
To this end, we utilize the following lemma.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}(a,b)=\exp(a^{\top}b/\gamma)
be the exponential kernel with a fixed
\gamma>0
. It holds for any
b\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}a\cdot\mathfrak{K}(a,b){\mathrm{d}}a=C_{1}\cdot b,

where
C_{1}>0
is an absolute constant.

Proof.

See §
B.5.1
for a detailed proof.
∎

By Lemma
B.2
, it holds for the right-hand side of (
B.5
) that

\displaystyle\iota\cdot C_{1}\cdot\frac{\sum^{L}_{\ell=1}\mathfrak{K}(k^{\ell},q)\cdot v^{\ell}}{\sum^{L}_{\ell=1}\mathfrak{K}(k^{\ell},q)}=\iota\cdot C_{1}\cdot V^{\top}\mathtt{softmax}(Kq/\gamma)=\iota\cdot C_{1}\cdot\mathtt{attn}_{\mathtt{SM}}(q,K,V),

where the first equality follows from the definition of the softmax function and the second equality follows from the definition of the softmax attention in (
4.10
).
By setting
C=\iota\cdot C_{1}
, we complete the proof of Proposition
4.2
.
∎

B.5.1
Proof of Lemma
B.2

Proof.

Let
a,b
be two vectors in the
(d-1)
-dimensional unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. We first define the following vector,

\displaystyle c=(a^{\top}b)\cdot b-\bigl(a-(a^{\top}b)\cdot b\bigr)\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

(B.14)

By direct calculation, we have the following property of
c
defined in (
B.14
),

\displaystyle c^{\top}b

\displaystyle=(a^{\top}b)\cdot\|b\|^{2}_{2}-a^{\top}b+(a^{\top}b)\cdot\|b\|^{2}_{2}=a^{\top}b.

(B.15)

By (
B.14
) and (
B.15
), we have that

\displaystyle a+c=2(a^{\top}b)\cdot b=2(c^{\top}b)\cdot b=(a^{\top}b)\cdot b+(c^{\top}b)\cdot b.

(B.16)

We now calculate the desired integration. Note that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}a\cdot\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a=b\cdot\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(a^{\top}b)\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(a-(a^{\top}b)\cdot b\bigr)\cdot\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a.

(B.17)

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
B.17
), it follows from (
B.14
) and (
B.15
) and (
B.16
) that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(a-(a^{\top}b)\cdot b\bigr)\cdot\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(c-(c^{\top}b)\cdot b\bigr)\cdot\exp(c^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(c-(c^{\top}b)\cdot b\bigr)\cdot\exp(c^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}c,

(B.18)

where the second equality follows from the fact that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}c=2\|b\|^{2}_{2}{\mathrm{d}}a-{\mathrm{d}}a={\mathrm{d}}a.

By replacing
c
by
a
on the right-hand side of (
B.5.1
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(a-(a^{\top}b)\cdot b\bigr)\cdot\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a=-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(a-(a^{\top}b)\cdot b\bigr)\cdot\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a=0

(B.19)

Finally, by plugging (
B.19
) into (
B.17
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}a\cdot\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a=b\cdot\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(a^{\top}b)\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a.

Thus, by setting

\displaystyle C_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(a^{\top}b)\exp(a^{\top}b){\mathrm{d}}a,\quad\forall b\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},

we complete the proof of Lemma
B.2
. Note that here
C_{1}
is an absolute constant that does not depend on
b
due to the symmetry on the unit sphere.
∎

Appendix C
Generalization Error Analysis

In this section, we analyze the generalization error of the complete setup of the transformer architecture, which involves multiple layers, skip connections, and multihead attentions. We collect the notations used throughout this section as follows.

Notations.
For two positive reals
r
and
s
such that
1/r+1/s=1
, we call
(r,s)
a conjugate pair. We denote by
\|\cdot\|_{r}
the vector
\ell_{r}
-norm when it operates on a vector. Let
M=(m_{1},\dots,m_{d_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
, where
m_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
with
i\in[d_{2}]
. We define the matrix
(r,s)
-norm as
\|M\|_{r,s}=(\sum_{i=1}^{d_{2}}\|m_{i}\|^{s}_{r})^{1/s}
. We define the
(r,s)
-operator norm as
\|M\|_{r\to s}=\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\|Mu\|_{s}/\|u\|_{r}
. We write
\|\cdot\|_{r}=\|\cdot\|_{r\to r}
when the
(r,r)
-operator norm operates on a matrix.

C.1
Complete Setup of Transformer Architecture

In what follows, we specify the complete setup of a
T
-layer transformer parameterized by
\theta=(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\theta^{(0)},\dots,\theta^{(T-1)})
, where the
t
-th layer (
t=0,\dots,T-1
) is parameterized by
\theta^{(t)}\in\Theta^{(t)}
and the aggregation layer is parameterized by
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. Here
\Theta^{(t)}
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
are the parameter spaces for the
t
-th layer and the aggregation layer, respectively. We define a two-layer feedforward neural network (FFN) with a skip connection (and no bias term) as follows,

\displaystyle{\mathtt{ffn}}(X;A)={\mathtt{ReLU}}(XA^{{\mathrm{x}}})A^{\sigma}+X\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d},

(C.1)

which is parameterized by
A=(A^{\mathrm{x}},A^{\sigma})
. Here
X\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
,
A^{{\mathrm{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\sigma}},A^{\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\sigma}\times d}
, and
{\mathtt{ReLU}}(\cdot)
is the rectified linear unit (ReLU) that operates elementwise. Corresponding to (
4.10
), we define the sequence-to-sequence counterpart of the softmax attention as follows,

\displaystyle\mathtt{attn}_{{\mathtt{SM}}}(Q,K,V)=\Bigl(V^{\top}\mathtt{norm}_{\mathtt{SM}}\bigl(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathtt{RBF}}(K,q^{\ell})\bigr)\Bigr)_{\ell\in[L]}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}.

(C.2)

Here
Q=(q^{\ell})_{\ell\in[L]}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
,
K=(k^{\ell})_{\ell\in[L]}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
, and
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathtt{RBF}}(K,q^{\ell})=(\mathfrak{K}_{\mathtt{RBF}}(q^{\ell},k^{\ell^{\prime}}))_{\ell^{\prime}\in[L]}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
is specified in Assumption
C.1
. Recall that
h
is the head number of the multihead attention defined in (
B.2
) and
d=d_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot h
. With a slight abuse of notations, we define the sequence-to-sequence counterpart of the multihead attention (MHA) as follows,

\displaystyle{\mathtt{mha}}(X;W)=\sum_{i=1}^{h}{\mathtt{head}}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}\mathtt{attn}_{\mathtt{SM}}(Q_{i},K_{i},V_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d},

(C.3)

which is parameterized by
W=\{(W^{{\mathrm{q}}}_{i},W^{{\mathrm{k}}}_{i},W^{{\mathrm{v}}}_{i})\}_{i\in[h]}
. Here
Q_{i}=XW^{\mathrm{q}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
,
K_{i}=XW^{\mathrm{k}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
, and
V_{i}=XW^{\mathrm{v}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
for the attention head
i\in[h]
, where
W^{\mathrm{q}}_{i},W^{\mathrm{k}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}
and
W^{\mathrm{v}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

With
X_{\star}^{(0)}=X
, let
X_{\star}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
be the intermediate input of the
t
-th layer (
t=0,\dots,T-1
) of the transformer architecture, which is defined as follows,

\displaystyle X^{(t)}

\displaystyle={\mathtt{ffn}}(X_{\star}^{(t)};A^{(t)}),\qquad

\displaystyle A^{(t)}

\displaystyle=(A^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)},A^{\sigma,(t)}),

\displaystyle X_{\star}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle={\mathtt{mha}}(X^{(t)};W^{(t)})+X^{(t)},\qquad

\displaystyle W^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(W^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i},W^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i},W^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i})\bigr\}_{i\in[h]},

(C.4)

Here
\theta^{(t)}=(A^{(t)},W^{(t)})
is the learnable parameter. We compute the output as follows,

\displaystyle\widehat{y}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(X^{(T)}_{\star})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{y}}},

(C.5)

where the aggregation layer
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}:\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\to\mathfrak{Y}
is parameterized by
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. Here
\mathfrak{Y}
is defined in (
5.2
). Note that
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
combines the aggregation layer
\mathtt{agg}_{\theta_{0}}
defined in (
5.1
) and the input mask
{\mathtt{msk}}
. For example, in the complete setup of ViT
(
Dosovitskiy et al. 2020
)
, the aggregation layer is the function composition
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(X^{(T)}_{\star})=\mathtt{agg}_{\theta_{0}}({\mathtt{mha}}(q_{W}({\mathtt{msk}}),X^{(T)}_{\star};W))
with
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=(\theta_{0},W)
, where
{\mathtt{msk}}
corresponds to the class encoding. Here the multihead attention
{\mathtt{mha}}(q_{W}({\mathtt{msk}}),X^{(T)}_{\star};W)
follows the definition in (
B.2
).

Empirical Image Class.
In what follows, we formalize the function class of the transformer architecture and the empirical image class for each layer. We define the base function class as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{mha}}^{L,(0)}=\bigl\{X^{(0)}_{\star}(X)\bigr\},

which is the function class that only contains the identity mapping. Here we use
X_{\star}^{(0)}(X)=X
to denote the identity mapping since
X^{(0)}_{\star}=X
. In the following, we use
X_{\star}^{(t)}(X)
and
X^{(t-1)}(X)
to denote the functions that map
X
to
X_{\star}^{(t)}
and
X^{(t)}
for
t=1,\dots,T-1
, respectively. We define the intermediate function classes recursively as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{L,(t)}_{\mathtt{ffn}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{{\mathtt{ffn}}\bigl(X_{\star}^{(t)}(X);A^{(t)}\bigr):A^{(t)}\in{\mathfrak{A}}^{(t)},X_{\star}^{(t)}(X)\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{mha}}^{L,(t)}\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{mha}}^{L,(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{{\mathtt{mha}}\bigl(X^{(t)}(X);W^{(t)}\bigr)+X^{(t)}:W^{(t)}\in{\mathfrak{W}}^{(t)},X^{(t)}(X)\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{ffn}}^{L,(t)}\Bigr\},

where
0\leq t\leq T-1
. Here
\Theta^{(t)}=\mathfrak{A}^{(t)}\times\mathfrak{W}^{(t)}
is the parameter space of the
t
-th layer of the transformer architecture. Correspondingly, we define the function class of the
T
-layer transformer as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{L}=\Bigl\{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\bigl(X_{\star}^{(T)}(X)\bigr):\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,X_{\star}^{(T)}(X)\in\mathcal{F}^{L,(T)}_{\mathtt{mha}}\Bigr\}.

(C.6)

Correspondingly, we define the empirical image classes as follows,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}(\mathcal{F}^{L,(t)}_{\mathtt{ffn}})

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\bigl(f(X_{i})^{\top}\bigr)_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times nL}:f\in\mathcal{F}^{L,(t)}_{\mathtt{ffn}}\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{mha}}^{L,(t+1)})

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\bigl(f(X_{i})^{\top}\bigr)_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times nL}:f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathtt{mha}}^{L,(t+1)}\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}(\mathcal{F}^{L})

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\bigl(f(X_{i})\bigr)_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{y}}\times n}:f\in\mathcal{F}^{L}\Bigr\},

(C.7)

where
0\leq t\leq T-1
.

C.2
Generalization Error Analysis for Complete Setup

In what follows, we present a general version of Theorem
5.3
, which allows for the complete setup of the transformer architecture. By specializing it to the single-layer transformer equipped with singlehead attention mechanism and no skip connection, we obtain Theorem
5.3
.

In parallel to (
5.4
), we make the following assumption on the Gaussian RBF kernel
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathtt{RBF}}(q,k)
, which induces the multihead attention
{\mathtt{mha}}(X;W)
defined in (
C.2
) and (
C.3
).

Assumption C.1

(Gaussian RBF Kernel)
.

Let
s>0
. We assume that the multihead attention
{\mathtt{mha}}(X;W)
adopts the Gaussian RBF kernel
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathtt{RBF}}(q,k)=\exp(-\|q-k\|_{2}^{2}/2\sigma^{2})
with
\sigma=(2d_{\mathrm{p}})^{1/s}
.

Note that the kernel function
\mathfrak{K}_{\mathtt{RBF}}(q,k)
in Assumption
C.1
is a general version of the Gaussian RBF kernel defined in (
5.4
), which corresponds to the special case where
s=2
. Recall that the output range
\mathfrak{Y}
is defined in (
5.2
). We make the following assumption on the aggregation layer
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
defined in (
C.5
).

Assumption C.2

(Aggregation Layer)
.

We assume that the aggregation layer
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}:\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{y}}}
has the output range
\mathfrak{Y}
. Let
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,j}:\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
be the
j
-th entry (
j\in[d_{\mathrm{y}}]
) of the aggregation function
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
. We assume that for any
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
,
X_{\star},\widetilde{X}_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times d}
, and
j\in[d_{\mathrm{y}}]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\bigl|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,j}(X_{\star})-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathtt{agg}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,j}(\widetilde{X}_{\star})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|X_{\star}^{\top}-\widetilde{X}_{\star}^{\top}\|_{r,\infty}.

Recall that
\|\cdot\|_{r}
denotes the
(r,r)
-operator norm when it operates on a matrix and
\|\cdot\|_{r,s}
is the matrix
(r,s)
-norm. In parallel to Assumption
5.1
, we make the following assumption on the parameter space for each layer of the transformer architecture.

Assumption C.3

(Parameter Space)
.

Let
(r,s)
be a conjugate pair. For
t=0,\dots,T-1
, we assume that the parameter space
\Theta^{(t)}={\mathfrak{A}}^{(t)}\times{\mathfrak{W}}^{(t)}
of the
t
-th layer of the transformer architecture satisfy

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{(A^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)},A^{\sigma,(t)})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\sigma}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\sigma}\times d}:

(C.8)

\displaystyle\qquad\bigl\|(A^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r}\leq{\alpha}^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)},\bigl\|(A^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r,s}\leq{R}^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)},\bigl\|(A^{\sigma,(t)})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r}\leq{\alpha}^{\sigma,(t)},\bigl\|(A^{\sigma,(t)})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r,s}\leq R^{\sigma,(t)}\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\bigl\{(W^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i},W^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i},W^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i})\bigr\}_{i\in[h]}:(W^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i},W^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i},W^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{p}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

\displaystyle\qquad\bigl\|(W^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r}\leq\omega^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i},\bigl\|(W^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r}\leq\omega^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i},\bigl\|(W^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r}\leq\omega^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i},

(C.9)

\displaystyle\qquad\bigl\|(W^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r,s}\leq R^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i},\bigl\|(W^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r,s}\leq R^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i},\bigl\|(W^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i})^{\top}\bigr\|_{r,s}\leq R^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i}\Bigr\}.

Also, we assume that the parameter space
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
of the aggregation layer takes the form of
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathtt{agg}}}:\|\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\theta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\|_{r}\leq 1\}
. Here
{\alpha}^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)},{R}^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)},{\alpha}^{\sigma,(t)},R^{\sigma,(t)},\omega^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i},\omega^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i},\omega^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i},R^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}_{i},R^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}_{i},R^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}_{i}>0
with
i\in[h]
and
t=0,\dots,T-1
.

To ease the presentation, we define the following quantities that combine the parameter bounds across the
h
heads within the
t
-th layer of the transformer architecture,

\displaystyle{\omega}^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{h}\omega_{i}^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)},\qquad\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\mathrm{q}}{\mathrm{k}},(t)}=\max_{i\in[h]}\{\omega_{i}^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}+\omega_{i}^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}\},

\displaystyle R^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{h}R_{i}^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)},\qquad{R}^{{\mathrm{q}}{\mathrm{k}},(t)}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}(R_{i}^{{\mathrm{q}},(t)}+R_{i}^{{\mathrm{k}},(t)}),

(C.10)

where
t=0,\dots,T-1
. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}^{(t)}=1+\alpha^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)}\alpha^{\sigma,(t)},\qquad\widetilde{\omega}^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}=1+\omega^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)},\qquad\gamma^{(t)}=\max\{\widetilde{\alpha}^{(t)},\widetilde{\omega}^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}\}.

(C.11)

Also, let

\displaystyle\kappa^{(t)}=\max\biggl\{\frac{\alpha^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)}R^{\sigma,(t)}+\alpha^{\sigma,(t)}R^{{\mathrm{x}},(t)}}{\widetilde{\alpha}^{(t)}},\frac{R^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}}{\widetilde{\omega}^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}},\frac{{R}^{{\mathrm{q}}{\mathrm{k}},(t)}}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\mathrm{q}}{\mathrm{k}},(t)}\omega^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}}\biggr\},\qquad\zeta^{(t)}=\frac{(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\omega\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\mathrm{q}}{\mathrm{k}},(t)})^{2}R^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}}{\widetilde{\omega}^{{\mathrm{v}},(t)}}.

(C.12)

Recall that the generalization error
\mathcal{E}_{\rm gen}
is defined in (
5.3
). The following theorem characterizes the generalization error of the transformer.

Theorem C.4

(Generalization Error of Transformer)
.

Let
D=\max\{d,d_{\sigma},d_{\mathrm{p}},d_{\mathrm{y}}\}
. Suppose that Assumptions
C.1
-
C.3
and Assumption
5.2
hold. Then, for any
\delta>0
, it holds with probability at least
1-\delta
that,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rm gen}=O\biggl(\frac{D^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\Bigl[T\sqrt{\log(1+\gamma)}+\sqrt{T}\sqrt{\log(1+\zeta R)}+\sqrt{\log(1+\kappa/\zeta)}\Bigr]\cdot\sqrt{hT}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\biggr),

where
R
is defined in (
5.2
). Here

\displaystyle\gamma=\max_{0\leq t\leq T-1}\gamma^{(t)},\qquad\kappa=\max_{0\leq t\leq T-1}\kappa^{(t)},\qquad\zeta=\max_{0\leq t\leq T-1}\zeta^{(t)},

(C.13)

where
\gamma^{(t)}
,
\kappa^{(t)}
, and
\zeta^{(t)}
are defined in (
C.11
)-(
C.12
).

Proof.

See §
C.4
for a detailed proof.
∎

Highlight.
In comparison with
Edelman et al. 2021
, we exploit the invariance/equivariance property of the transformer architecture in a fine grained manner. The key observation is that, due to the invariance/equivariance property, the dimensions of the learnable parameters
W^{(t)}
and
A^{(t)}
are independent of the sequence length
L
. By such an observation, we characterize the covering number of the function class with the covering numbers of the parameter spaces and propagate them through the
T
layers. As a consequence, the covering number of the function class is independent of
L
. In contrast, the generalization error in
Edelman et al. 2021
has a logarithmic dependency on
L
.

Interpretation.
We interpret the generalization error in Theorem
C.4
as follows. On the one hand, the
O(1/\sqrt{n}
dependencies in
O(\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n})
and
O(D^{2}/\sqrt{n})
over the sample size
n
are standard in the literature. On the other hand, the
O(D^{2}h^{1/2}T^{3/2})
scaling implies that the transformer architecture requires more training data points to generalize as the dimension
D
of the parameter space, the number
T
of layers, and the head number
h
grow. Also, the generalization error in Theorem
C.4
only scales logarithmically in
\gamma
,
\kappa
, and
\zeta
